### 2026-08-19 — Mark's REM feedback adds Batch 221

Mark's most important point that morning: *"App continually flags low rem in sleep and says
worth tracking but doesn't actually track it. Would be really helpful if it could track
patterns / movement and any obvious causes so I can see what things to try and if they're
working."* His screenshot shows the brief flagging **REM 9.8% against a 15–23% age band** and
saying "worth tracking" — then, one line later, "*worth noting whether that pattern appeared
last night*", **asking Mark to observe something the app holds the hypnogram to compute.**

Reconciled against production, this is not one gap but a loop broken in three places, and
only the first was already planned:

* **REM is not an outcome.** Covered by Batch 219.5 and analysed by 220.
* **No nightly observation is ever recorded.** Re-verified 2026-08-24: all three standing
  experiments — collagen, recovery-week disruption, and the 04:00 waking pattern — still hold
  `observations: {"entries": []}` and status `active`. Batch 220 can now append an occasional
  longitudinal temperature finding to the early-waking experiment, but its first paid run is
  correctly alert-gated and production still has zero entries; it is not a per-night loop.
* **Evaluation results never reach the brief.** `experiment_evaluation.py` has real
  evaluators (a gate for collagen, a **Pearson correlation** for `early_waking_0400`, a
  group-compare) and production still has exactly **21 stored evaluation audits** — but the
  current engine has moved on: on 2026-08-24 it reads collagen `supported` (9-night gate),
  recovery-week `inconclusive`, and early-waking `inconclusive`. The Experiments page and
  handover can show those reads, and Batch 187 recomputes them for state-change detection;
  `experiments` is still **absent from the morning packet entirely**, so the morning coach
  cannot see any of them.

And the sharpest one, which nothing covers: **`rem_interventions.py` (Batch 72) hands Mark a
rotating library of 12 REM levers every week and contains no measurement of any kind** — the
module selects and renders strings, while `RemRotation` carries only period/shown/total and
therefore cannot even identify which levers were issued. `manual_entries` has no field for
whether he applied one; inferring that from notes would turn prose into controlled evidence.
He has been told to try things, weekly, with no feedback loop ever closing. Batch 221 closes
it. **Sequencing corrected 2026-08-24:** Batches 219 and 220 are both shipped; 221 consumes
219's REM/awake outcomes and the same audited experiment rail 220 now targets.

| Batch | Tier | Status | Phases | Goal | Acceptance criteria |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~Batch 221 — The experiment loop closes: what was tried, and did it work~~ | 🔴 High | Shipped | **Corrected 2026-08-24 at `/batch-start`, before product code.** The intent stands, but sequencing and three implementation facts had decayed: Batch 220 already ships one idempotent longitudinal observation route; the 21 stored evaluations are not the current state and the engine is also consumed by Experiments, handover and Batch 187; and the REM rotation exposes rendered strings with no lever IDs, while `manual_entries` has no application-evidence field. A final boundary check also found `ChronicSuggestionItemOut` drops the service's existing `rotation` object before Daily Loop reaches the shared schema/UI, so even period/shown/total is absent in production.<br>221.1 **Record nightly evidence, not duplicate the evaluators.** Keep the shipped gate/correlation/group-compare engines and human-gated terminal conclusion exactly as they are. Add an idempotent forward-only nightly snapshot per wake date to each of the three standing experiments: collagen gets the scored-night/gate evidence, recovery-week gets the plan-block group plus sleep outcome, and early-waking gets awake/REM outcomes plus the measured drivers/setup available that night. Batch 220's occasional longitudinal finding remains a separate source in the same entries array. Do not fabricate historical application evidence; on the first run begin with the current wake date, then fill genuine post-deploy gaps after downtime.<br>221.2 **Surface the current evaluation, not a stale audit.** Add the active experiment state and a read-only evaluation recomputed as of the packet's subject date to the morning context packet, with the latest stored audit identified only as provenance. On 2026-08-24 production already reads collagen `supported` (9 consecutive 74+ nights), recovery-week `inconclusive` (−1.2 points; 14 recovery / 36 build nights), and early-waking `inconclusive` (weak strongest signal, `r=-0.3267`, 10 paired fan-speed nights); the morning read sees none of them today. The brief reports an actionable supported/refuted result, otherwise says exactly why it is inconclusive/insufficient, and never auto-concludes.<br>221.3 **Make the REM issue and application joinable.** Give every selected REM action its stable library ID and persist one immutable weekly assignment containing the exact IDs and rendered copy Mark was shown. Seed a fourth standing experiment, `rem_intervention_rotation`, for this protocol rather than mislabelling REM evidence as the 04:00 hypothesis. Migration `030` adds `manual_entries.rem_intervention_feedback_json`: an optional structured `applied` / `not_applied` / unknown response for only the assignment that covered the night ending on that entry's wake date. This explicit check-in evidence is required because application cannot be derived honestly from Garmin, setup or prose. Pair it with Batch 219's stored REM minutes, awake minutes and sleep duration/percentage in the nightly observation. A Monday check-in refers to Sunday night's prior assignment, never the new Monday rotation.<br>221.4 **Evaluate slowly and say so.** Add a deterministic descriptive evaluator for the REM experiment. Until the same lever has the configured applied/comparison coverage, its recommendation is `inconclusive` and names the remaining nights; it never turns one night's direction into a claim. Once sampled, it surfaces applied-vs-not-applied REM/awake summaries with explicit observational confounds; the existing human conclusion gate remains the only terminal write.<br>221.5 **Close the promise loop in both surfaces.** The check-in shows only genuinely issued prior-night levers and lets Mark record application; the morning packet carries assignment, outcome and evaluation. Prompt policy forbids asking him to notice REM/awake facts the hypnogram already supplies and permits asking only for user-owned application evidence. The existing chronic card reads the persisted current-week assignment so the copy being measured is byte-for-byte the copy that was issued.<br>221.6 Tests: forward-only and gap-filling nightly observation writes for all three standing hypotheses; Batch 220's different-source observation survives; current evaluation reaches the morning packet while status stays active; weekly assignment round-trips stable IDs/copy; Sunday-night feedback survives a Monday rotation boundary; unknown is not silently treated as not-applied; an underpowered REM comparison is inconclusive; the check-in response round-trips; and prompt tests prohibit outsourcing derivable REM/awake observations to Mark. | Mark can see which REM lever he was actually given, record whether he applied it, and see what happened afterward; the app's three existing hypotheses accumulate nightly evidence and the morning coach stops hiding evaluations or asking Mark to inspect data it already holds. | Nightly observations accumulate idempotently against all three original standing hypotheses without changing their shipped evaluators or status; the new REM protocol experiment holds immutable weekly assignments and wake-date application/outcome observations; migration `030` provides structured application evidence rather than mining prose; current evaluations reach the morning packet and never auto-conclude; the displayed chronic action matches the persisted assignment; an underpowered REM comparison is explicitly inconclusive and names the coverage still needed. Depends on shipped Batch 219 outcomes and interoperates with Batch 220’s distinct longitudinal-observation source. **Shipped via PR #253 / squash `f976d13`.** Migration `030`, immutable stable-ID/exact-copy weekly assignments, explicit prior-night application feedback, four idempotent source-owned nightly observation streams, current read-only morning evaluations and the conservative human-gated REM comparison shipped together. Local backend passed 939 tests; shared/web passed 30/394; static, build and Chromium accessibility gates passed. Final PostgreSQL CI passed **1315 tests / 0 skipped**. Production freshness and Railway/Vercel health are green on exact SHA `f976d1307f1f5fea7ee5460ebaca3c5ae5a39dda`; a rollback-only deployed smoke exercised Alembic `030`, assignment, all four writes and packet evaluation with no persisted smoke data. Decision #299. |

### 2026-08-20 — The check-in beat the sync: Batch 222

Craig reported that Thursday 20 August "*is saying there is no overnight data*". It was, and
**the packet was honest** — at generation time `coach.sleep` and `coach.daily_metrics` held
**no row at all** for 2026-08-20. The model narrated a real absence. The failure is entirely
upstream, and it is a race that then locked itself in.

**Reconstructed to the second from the `wake_check` audit row, `job_runs` counters,
`generation_requests` and `manual_entries` — not inferred:**

| UTC | Event |
|---|---|
| 07:12:15 | Mark's sleep session ends (08:12 local) |
| 07:19:41 | `wake_check` first sees `sleepEnd=07:12:15` — 7m26s old against `SETTLE_MIN = 20` — and correctly returns `wait` / `awaiting_stability` |
| **07:26:55** | **Mark saves his morning check-in** |
| 07:27:47 | Brief generated on a packet with no overnight rows |
| 07:34:39 | The poll that would have fired (stable **and** settled at 22m24s) instead skips: `waiting: 1` → `waiting: 0 / fired: 0` |

He missed a correct read by about seven minutes, and the brief he got then cancelled the sync
that would have corrected it. Garmin had the night all along: bed 00:23 → wake 08:12, 7h04m
asleep, score 78, HRV 44.

**Three defects stacked, and the second is the serious one:**

* **The check-in generates with no freshness gate.** `upsert_manual_entry`
  (`daily_loop.py:1436-1441`) schedules `_generate_brief_after_checkin` on any same-day
  check-in without checking whether the day's inputs have synced — and that path reads stored
  rows only; it never syncs.
* **An empty read disables the day's sync.** `run_wake_check` short-circuits at
  `scheduler.py:1000` on `latest_analysis(...) is not None`, conflating *the day has been
  read* with *the day has been synced*. A brief that read nothing therefore cancels the very
  sync that would have filled it, for the rest of the day.
* **The backstop re-syncs but never re-reads.** `run_morning_weather_sync` calls
  `_sync_morning_inputs` unconditionally, so the 11:00 local backstop *would* have landed the
  data — but `generate_and_store` (`morning_analysis.py:740-748`) returns the existing
  analysis whenever `prompt_version` matches, and `morning_generation_identity` hashes the
  check-in and the prompt version and **nothing about whether the packet had any data**. Left
  alone, the day ends with correct numbers on the dashboard beside a brief insisting there are
  none.

**Blast radius measured, not assumed: 2026-08-20 is the only date in 60 days with a morning
brief and no sleep row.** Every other morning the wake job won the race, which is why this has
survived unnoticed since Batch 85 split the morning at its sync → generate seam.

| Batch | Tier | Status | Phases | Goal | Acceptance criteria |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~Batch 222 — A brief is never generated on a day the app has not synced~~ | 🔴 High | Shipped | **Corrected 2026-08-24 at `/batch-start`, before product code.** The three defects and the 2026-08-20 reconstruction still hold exactly, and a read-only production recheck still finds exactly one null-sleep/null-metrics morning packet in the 60-day window (`d03b45f2…`) beside its repaired complete successor (`34be38d4…`). The cited lines have shifted after Batches 219–221: the background helper is now `daily_loop.py:170`, the router schedules it at `:1522-1562`, the wake short-circuit is `scheduler.py:1080`, and the hero copy is `GoodMorningCta.tsx:43`; that component is shared by Home **and Sleep**, so both callers must stay honest. **Design call resolved at batch start:** use sync-then-generate in the existing off-request task. A current-date morning `DailyMetric` is the durable proof that the full Garmin pull completed; before the 11:00 backstop the wake poll also requires the `Sleep` row and therefore keeps repairing a lagging sleep record, while after the backstop a completed Garmin pull may honestly establish that no sleep record exists. Encode daily-metric/sleep presence in the generation identity so a materially more complete packet is grounds for a new read, while retaining the earlier analysis as history.<br>222.1 **What Mark saw (2026-08-20).** The brief opened "*No sleep data recorded for last night*" and told him Garmin "hasn't returned a sleep record" — while Garmin had recorded a complete night. Verified: the packet's `sleep` and `dailyMetrics` keys were both `null`, so the model reported an absence faithfully. **Do not treat this as a prompt or model problem.**<br>222.2 **Close the freshness gap at the check-in.** `upsert_manual_entry` (`daily_loop.py:1436-1441`) schedules generation with no gate on whether today's inputs exist, and `_generate_brief_after_checkin` → `regenerate_after_morning_checkin` reads stored rows only. Decide between the two honest remedies and take one: **sync-then-generate** (the input sync is idempotent, so the check-in can pull the day before reading it) or **hold** — refuse to generate and leave the client on the "Syncing your overnight data" stage the check-in page already owns (`CheckInPage.tsx:121`). Silently reading an empty day is the one option that must stop.<br>222.3 **Stop an empty read from cancelling the day's sync — the defect with the widest consequence.** `run_wake_check` (`scheduler.py:1000`) skips a profile once `latest_analysis(...)` is non-null, which conflates *read* with *synced*. Gate it on the day's inputs actually being present, so a brief generated early can never be the thing that prevents the data arriving. **Guard against the opposite failure in the same phase:** the short-circuit exists to stop the job polling all day, so the replacement must still terminate once the day is genuinely synced.<br>222.4 **Decide, explicitly, whether a materially-more-complete packet is grounds to re-read.** Today it is not: the generation identity hashes the check-in and the prompt version only, so the 11:00 backstop re-syncs and then serves the stale read. If the answer is yes, the identity must be able to express packet completeness; if no, then 222.2/222.3 must guarantee the empty read never happens, because nothing downstream will repair it. **This is the batch's one real design call — resolve it at `/batch-start`, not mid-build.**<br>222.5 **The copy makes a promise nothing checks.** Home's morning rung 0 offers "Say good morning" whenever `morningAnalysis == null` (`homeActions.ts:147`), and `GoodMorningCta` states as fact "*your overnight data's already in*" (`GoodMorningCta.tsx:41`) — true only because the wake job usually wins the race. Batch 85's own comment records the assumption. Either verify the claim before making it, or let the check-in path make it true.<br>222.6 Tests: a check-in on a day with no synced sleep/metrics must not produce a no-data brief, under whichever remedy 222.2 chooses; `run_wake_check` must still fire when today's analysis exists but the day's inputs do not, **and** must still stop polling once they do; a wake-detector regression reproducing the real 07:12:15 / 07:19:41 / 07:26:55 ordering that fails on the pre-fix path; the Home rung and hero asserted against a synced-vs-unsynced day. | The app never tells Mark his night was not recorded when Garmin recorded it — and a brief generated early can never be the thing that stops the data arriving. | A check-in landing before wake-detection either waits for the sync or triggers it, and never yields a no-data read; wake-detection is not cancelled by an analysis that read nothing, and still terminates once the day is synced; the 07:26:55 ordering is pinned as a regression. **Production was repaired on the day (Craig's call, 2026-08-20).** `run_scheduled morning-sync` landed the Garmin day (08-20 `morning` metrics + sleep, and 08-19's missing `settled` row), the pinned generation identity was released, and `regenerate_after_morning_checkin` re-read on the real packet: new analysis `34be38d4…` at 08:58:04Z (bed 00:23 → wake 08:12, score 78 / age-adjusted 82, readiness 73), with the empty-packet row `d03b45f2…` **retained as history** and a `morning_brief_repair` audit row recording before/after, reason and method. **This departs from Batches 212/214/215, which each declined a production repair** on the principle that a stored analysis records what the coach actually said — the distinction is deliberate: those recorded a wrong *judgement* on real data, whereas this recorded an *absence of data that was never real*. **Two gotchas from the repair.** (1) `run_scheduled morning-sync` run locally via `railway run` **hung after committing the per-date syncs**, leaving an orphaned backend idle-in-transaction holding a granted advisory lock — which would have blocked the app's own generation path, not just the repair. Check `pg_locks` for `locktype='advisory'` after any killed `railway run`, and terminate the backend. In-container it completes in ~20 s. (2) `force=True` on `generate_and_store` is **not** sufficient to re-read: it skips the `latest_analysis` check but not the `claim_generation_request` identity check, so a genuine regeneration also needs the completed `generation_requests` row released. **Shipped via PR #254 / squash `4292339`.** The check-in and 11:00 backstop now sync then enforce persisted input readiness before any model call; wake polling terminates on a morning `DailyMetric` plus pre-cutoff `Sleep`, never on an analysis; completeness-aware identities preserve an empty historical read but generate again when its inputs become complete; and Home/Sleep make the same verified readiness claim. Final PostgreSQL CI passed **1322 tests / 0 skipped**. Production freshness and Railway/Vercel health are green on exact SHA `4292339eaab31f8ade4cfe80b842c3d73cb94c0e`; read-only deployed-container smoke proved all pre-/post-cutoff readiness, ordering and identity boundaries with no Garmin, model, notification or database write. Decision #300. |

### 2026-08-20 — Found during Batch 215 closeout: Batch 223

Batch 215 needed a test helper that reads the *working* interval rather than the hottest
step, because Mark's 2026-08-08 ride warms up to 65% and works at 62% — so an assertion
about "the working intensity" was silently checking the warm-up ramp. Chasing that back
into source found the same shape doing real work, and a trap sitting on top of it.

`structured_workout_builder.py:133` computes `max_power` over **every expanded step**, then
hands it to `_workout_type_for_power` (`:275`) and `_intensity_target_for_power` (`:287`).
A warm-up ramp is therefore allowed to name the session. Measured against production — all
86 active structured bike workouts, driven through the real expander and the real
classifier — **30 have a warm-up or cool-down hotter than their working step, and on 14 of
those it changes the classified type**: `bike_endurance` → `bike_tempo`, off nothing more
than a standard 80% warm-up ramp on a 67% Zone-2 ride.

**It is latent, not live, and that distinction is the whole priority argument.** Every one
of those 30 rows came from `plan_no2_import` (25), `interval_editor` (3), `holiday_pause`
(1) or `today_card_swap` (1), all of which carry the type through from the plan. The
classifier only runs on the freeform custom-workout path, which writes
`source="plan_action_add"` (`plan_actions.py:753`) — a value that appears **nowhere** in the
divergent set. Nothing is mislabelled today. The next freeform Zone-2 ride would be.

**The trap is the more valuable half of this row, and it would have been shipped as the
fix.** Nine live rows are stored `bike_endurance` while containing **185% FTP** work, and
they are *correct*: they are the `Z2 + Neuromuscular` sessions. The data separates cleanly
on duration, not power — every `bike_vo2` row's hot steps run **30–180 s at 108–130%**,
while every sprint row is **6 × 12 s at 185%**. A power-only classifier cannot tell a
12-second neuromuscular sprint from a four-minute VO2 interval, so "just classify on the
working step" would flip all nine into `bike_vo2` and feed the weekly mix a phantom VO2
session — **Batch 213's complaint, inverted**, on the same nine Saturdays Mark rides.

Safety is unaffected either way and must not be cited as a reason to hurry: `blocks_red_vo2`
reads the IR, and Batch 203 already moved morning Red/VO2 detection onto a buildable IR with
the type name only as fallback. What rides on `workout_type` is *accounting* —
`weekly_mix.mix_bucket` (`:89`) maps it to a bucket, and `categorize`/`category_for_workout_type`
scope swaps and week views.

| Batch | Tier | Status | Phases | Goal | Acceptance criteria |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~Batch 223 — A session is named by the work it asks for, not by its warm-up~~ | 🟢 Mid | Shipped | **Corrected 2026-08-24 at `/batch-start`, before product code.** The defect remains latent, but provenance and blast-radius facts had decayed. Production still has 86 active structured bike rows: 30 have a hotter warm-up/cool-down, now split 24 `plan_no2_import` / 3 `interval_editor` / 2 `today_card_swap` / 1 `holiday_pause`; 14 actually cross a type boundary (10 / 2 / 1 / 1 respectively). `build_freeform_bike_workout` is called by both add and edit, and edit preserves the prior row source, so `source="plan_action_add"` cannot prove the classifier ran. Its own durable payload marker is `structuredWorkout.source="structured_builder"`, and a read-only production scan finds zero active **or historical** rows carrying it; the 10 historical / 5 active `plan_action_add` rows all use predefined templates. The nine 12-second 185% sprint rows and 30–180-second VO2 separation still hold exactly, but the current router, shared schema and web editor accept integer-minute work legs only, so sub-minute primer classification is a defensive rule for the expanded-step classifier rather than a currently reachable freeform path. Rechecking five canonical fixtures across base/Amber/Red produced 15 cases; only the three `MARK_0808_STRUCTURED` variants differ between any-step and working-step maxima (65% warm-up vs 62% work).<br>223.1 **The defect, measured rather than reported.** `build_freeform_bike_workout` takes `max_power` across every expanded step (`structured_workout_builder.py:132-138`) and classifies on it. Of 86 active structured bike workouts in production, 30 carry a warm-up/cool-down hotter than their working step and 14 would be retyped `bike_endurance` → `bike_tempo` by an 80% warm-up ramp alone.<br>223.2 **Confirm the blast radius before widening the fix.** Use the builder's own `structuredWorkout.source="structured_builder"` marker for the blast-radius check; `planned_workouts.source` is inherited on edit and cannot answer whether classification ran. Re-verify both active and historical rows at build time — if a builder-marked row has since been created, this stops being latent and the row's priority changes.<br>223.3 **Classify on the working steps** — the `phase == "interval"` subset, matching `verdict_scaling._primary_work_step`, which already gets this right and is the model to copy. Warm-up and cool-down describe how the session is entered and left, not what it asks of him.<br>223.4 **Do not stop there, or this row ships a worse bug than it fixes.** Nine live rows are `bike_endurance` with 185% FTP work and they are correct — `Z2 + Neuromuscular`. Working-step power alone retypes all nine to `bike_vo2`. **The expanded-step classifier must remain duration-aware even though the current minute-only editor cannot author these sprint legs:** production separates the two classes without ambiguity — VO2 hot steps run 30–180 s at 108–130%, neuromuscular sprints run 6 × 12 s at 185% — so a short supra-threshold burst inside an otherwise easy ride is a primer, not the session's purpose. Pick the threshold from that gap and say why in the code.<br>223.5 **Say what is *not* in scope.** `blocks_red_vo2` and the Batch 203 morning VO2 detection read the IR, so no safety property depends on this; the consumer that does is accounting — `weekly_mix.mix_bucket` (`:89`) and the category-scoped swap/week views. No backfill of the 86 existing rows: their stored types are already right, and rewriting them would be a change with no defect behind it.<br>223.6 **The parallel finding, recorded so it is not re-derived.** The test helper `_max_power` in `test_executable_coaching.py` reads every step; `_max_work_power` was added in Batch 215 for assertions about working intensity. Measured across the five canonical fixtures in base, Amber and Red forms (15 cases), only the three 2026-08-08 forms diverge — so no pre-existing assertion was wrong, and this phase is a naming/guard cleanup rather than a bug fix.<br>223.7 Tests: the nine `Z2 + Neuromuscular` rows as a committed fixture that must stay `bike_endurance`; a Zone-2 ride with an 80% warm-up ramp must not classify as tempo; a real VO2 shape (5 × 2 min @ 120%) must still classify `bike_vo2`; a duration boundary case either side of the chosen threshold. | A session is named by the work it asks for, so the weekly mix counts what Mark actually rode rather than what his warm-up touched. | A Zone-2 ride with a hot warm-up classifies as endurance; a genuine VO2 session still classifies as VO2; the nine neuromuscular Saturdays stay endurance and never become a phantom VO2 session in the weekly mix; no existing row is rewritten. **Priority: low, and honestly so** — this is latent on a path Mark has not used, and the reason to write it down now is 223.4, which is not recoverable from the code. **Shipped via PR #255 / squash `0427bb7`.** Sustained interval work now owns type and weekly accounting; ≤20-second supra-threshold primers retain an honest peak target without becoming VO2, while all-step Red safety is unchanged. Final PostgreSQL CI passed **1328 tests / 0 skipped**. Production freshness and Railway/Vercel health are green on exact SHA `0427bb7d6631bfb51eab4e95d86eb37983471a25`; read-only deployed-container smoke reproduced hot-warm-up Z2, both duration-boundary cases, neuromuscular Z2 and genuine VO2 with their independent Red blocks. No existing row was rewritten. Decision #301. |

### 2026-08-23 — Found during Batch 217 `/batch-start`: Batch 224

Batch 216 shipped the settled-row read for Body Battery on 2026-08-20 and its smoke passed
honestly. **Mark's reported symptom is nonetheless unchanged: on a live morning both rows are
still blank.** Measured on today's real packet (2026-08-23, generated 08:34 UTC),
`metricsVsBaselines` holds `body_battery_charge.currentValue: null` and
`body_battery_drain.currentValue: null` against fully-populated baselines (means 65.2 and
64.6, 83 samples each), and the brief's metrics table therefore omits both rows entirely —
it lists Body Battery *end* and nothing else. He reported this twice (08-15, 08-19).

**This is not a mistake in Batch 216; it is the boundary of what 216 fixed, and the row
should say so.** `_day_aggregate_metric` prefers the `phase="settled"` row and falls back to
the morning row, and `complete_body_battery_charged()` then refuses the morning row for
incomplete local-day coverage. 216's own smoke asserted exactly that ("a partial total
correctly withheld"). But the settled row is written in the evening — 20:07, 20:28 and 21:14
UTC on 08-20/21/22 — and the morning brief is generated at 07:27-09:19. **There is never a
settled row for the subject date when the brief runs**, so the fallback is the only path a
live morning ever takes, and it always withholds.

**The two metrics are not the same case, and that is the whole design question.** Charge
accrues overnight and is essentially complete by wake: the morning and settled rows agree
closely (08-22 61/61, 08-20 67/67, 08-21 78/81). Drain does not: morning 5-23 against
settled 57-74 on the same dates. So comparing a morning-row *charge* to the settled-row
baseline is near apples-to-apples, while doing the same for *drain* would tell Mark he is
drastically below his normal every single morning — the false-fact failure Batch 214 named
as worse than a missing one. Any fix must split them rather than lifting the gate on both.

| Batch | Tier | Status | Phases | Goal | Acceptance criteria |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~Batch 224 — Overnight charge reaches the morning brief; drain waits for the day it describes~~ | 🟢 Mid | Shipped | **Corrected 2026-08-24 at `/batch-start`, before product code.** The latest live packet is now 2026-08-24 at 09:26 UTC: both Body Battery baseline rows carry `currentValue: null`, while its stored morning fields are charge 69, drain 10, end 97 and stress 14. The shared `MetricComparisonTable` does not drop null rows; it renders each as an em dash while still showing the full baseline range, which is the user-visible ambiguity. The generated prose already calls the 69-point overnight recharge out from raw `dailyMetrics`, so this batch reconciles two contradictory packet views rather than revealing a wholly absent fact. Across all 63 dates holding morning and settled charge, 37 match exactly and 60 are within 6 points (median absolute difference 0, mean 3.48); the only three large gaps are 2026-06-22..24 at 50–53 points, when the old fixed 05:30 analysis ran 96–111 minutes before recorded wake. All eight genuinely live phase-keyed pairs since 2026-08-16 differ by 0–4 points, four exactly. `body_battery_end` is deliberately valid as the point-in-time wake value and its separate whole-day consumer already uses `complete_body_battery_end`; stress average, like drain, is partial at wake, but has no current baseline row and its whole-day consumer is already coverage-gated.<br>224.1 **The symptom, measured not reported.** Confirm against a live packet that `body_battery_charge` and `body_battery_drain` both render `currentValue: null` on a morning brief and that the shared table renders both rows as em dashes beside their populated full-day baseline ranges. Confirm the cause is the settled-row-first fallback landing on a morning row that `complete_body_battery_charged` refuses, **not** a missing value — the 2026-08-23 morning row holds `body_battery_charged: 75`.<br>224.2 **Decide charge on its own evidence.** Use the corrected all-date measurement above: 60/63 pairs are within 6 points, and every post-wake-detection live pair is within 4; only if that is close enough to compare against a settled-row baseline, admit the morning row for charge with a basis naming it as the overnight figure (Batch 217's convention, which will have shipped). If they do not agree, say so and leave it withheld — a wrong current value beside a real baseline is worse than a blank.<br>224.3 **Leave drain withheld on the morning read, and say why in the packet rather than silently omitting the row.** Morning drain is a few hours of a day that has not happened; the baseline is a full-day figure. A blank with no explanation is what sent Mark to the chat in the first place, so the row should state that the day's drain is not yet countable.<br>224.4 **Check the sibling metrics for the same live-morning blind spot.** `body_battery_end` is exempt rather than lucky: at wake it is the current point-in-time level, while `_yesterday_load_packet` separately requires a complete row before calling it a finished-day end. `stress_avg` has the same partial-window shape as drain, but no metrics-vs-baselines row consumes it; its only whole-day consumer is already coverage-gated. Keep those boundaries explicit and unchanged.<br>224.5 Tests: a live-morning fixture (morning row only, no settled row for the subject date) asserting the chosen charge behaviour and the drain withhold; the existing Batch 216 settled-row assertions must stay green byte-for-byte; a regression pinning that the metrics table never renders a morning-window drain against a full-day baseline. | Mark sees his overnight Body Battery charge on the morning that describes it, and where a figure genuinely is not countable yet the app says so instead of showing nothing. | Charge is present on a live morning brief or its absence is explained; drain is never compared against a full-day baseline from a part-day window; every Batch 216 assertion survives unchanged. **Priority note:** this is the only remaining live user-visible regression from Mark's 08-15/08-19 reports, and the third time this metric has been through the ledger — 205 split the rows, 216 taught the reader to prefer the settled one, and neither reaches the morning he actually reads. **Shipped via PR #256 / squash `711055a`: strict settled-row lookup; proven partial-window morning charge with basis; part-day drain withheld with a visible reason; optional shared fields; settled and sibling behavior unchanged. Final PostgreSQL CI 1330 passed; Chromium mobile/desktop and exact-SHA deployed-code smokes green. Decision #302.** |

### Future enhancements — recorded, not scheduled

Authored here so the reasoning survives, each with the trigger that would make it due. **None
of these is blocked; all are deliberately deferred on value-per-batch at a 1–2-user scale.**

* **Tool use / the agent loop (Claude can verify its own inputs).** The full fix for
  "disconnected": read-only tools — `get_interval_detail`, `get_planned_week`,
  `get_metric_baseline`, `explain_tag` — so that when Mark says "*not sure where you're
  getting this from*" the coach can go and look, and tell him the app is wrong. Requires
  adopting the Python SDK's tool runner in place of the single POST. **This is where the
  entire 2–4× cost increase lives** (an agent loop re-sends the whole conversation each
  iteration: a 13K packet with four tool calls is ~75K input where today it is 13K), plus the
  largest build cost — tool definitions, iteration caps, and a safety review of what Claude
  may read. **Why deferred, and why the case genuinely weakens:** tools are insurance against
  a class of bug that Batches 212–217 largely eliminate. After them the VO2 target is right,
  the sprints are not misgraded, and the illness tag does not fire — so the value of "Claude
  can go and check" drops sharply. **Trigger:** a *new* class of error that 212–217 did not
  catch, or Mark still reporting the app feels disconnected after 217 and 220 have been live
  for a few weeks. At that point the complaint will name exactly what to build.
* **Claude replaces regex classification of Mark's own words.** The deeper fix behind Batch
  212: `_CHECK_IN_CAUSE_PATTERNS` does natural-language classification with regex across five
  cause families, and no amount of pattern-tightening makes that safe — the next failure is
  just a different word. A model reads "*more awake as felt cold from drafts*" correctly.
  **Trigger:** a second over-match reaching Mark after 212, or Batch 220 proving out, since
  the analyst run is already reading his notes longitudinally and is the natural home for it.
* **Anthropic SDK adoption.** Would replace the hand-rolled `classify_anthropic_error` with
  typed exceptions, and unlock structured outputs (`output_config.format` — Batch 143 existed
  to fix a chat/post-workout JSON-500 that the current join-the-text-blocks approach makes
  possible by design) and streaming (Batch 144's "orphaned generating spinner" is the UX
  streaming removes; the client is non-streaming on a 60 s timeout). **Trigger:** the tools
  work, which needs it anyway — or a third JSON-parse defect.
* **Model review — `claude-sonnet-4-6` → Sonnet 5.** A dated decision (paid API chosen for
  health-data privacy; "keep Sonnet 4.6, revisit ~Sept 2026") now nearly due. Sonnet 5 is
  materially better on agentic work at the same tier. **Not a drop-in:** adaptive thinking is
  on by default, non-default sampling parameters are rejected, and the tokenizer produces
  ~30% more tokens for the same text, so `max_tokens` and cost baselines shift. **Trigger:**
  the September review, or the tools work — tool-triggering behaviour is where the
  generational gap is largest.
* **Room cooling beyond the fan.** Today the thermal model is one scalar and one actuator, and
  it is **one-directional**: `ON_THRESHOLD_C = 19.5` / `OFF_THRESHOLD_C = 19.0`
  (`fan_control.py:52`) can only cool, so both of Mark's recent failures — cold-side, 08-14
  and 08-15 — are events the autopilot cannot represent. Candidate levers: a computed
  pre-cool crossover time (he guesses 18:30), an earlier control window, cold-side thresholds
  and a draught alert, cross-ventilation guidance from wind direction, dawn planning from the
  hourly curve, and daytime solar-gain exclusion. **Shape matters:** the app cannot open a
  window, so this is an evening *cooling plan* nudge, not an extension of the autopilot.
  **Trigger:** Batch 220 identifying an optimum worth actively steering toward — advice
  without a target is guesswork.
* **Cooling Mark rather than the room.** Sleep onset is driven by core temperature *falling*,
  achieved by dumping heat through the skin — mainly hands and feet — via vasodilation. An
  over-cold room triggers vasoconstriction and *impairs* that, which is the physiological
  reason an optimum exists and the reason both his cold nights failed. The most app-native
  lever is **workout timing versus bedtime** (training elevates core temperature for hours;
  the app already owns every session time, and this sits directly on top of Batch 215's
  sleep-pressure argument — sleep pressure and residual thermal load pull in opposite
  directions late in the evening). Others: a warm shower ~1–2 h before bed, feet outside the
  covers as a heat dump that needs no cold room, and bedding/mattress microclimate. Every one
  is a natural fit for the existing `experiments` lifecycle. **Trigger:** Batch 219/220
  proving the logging and findings rails work — these should be *tested* on Mark, not
  asserted at him.
* **Widen the findings-to-state channel.** Batch 220.7 opens it for analyst findings only.
  The general form is that any Claude conclusion — from chat, from a post-workout read —
  can become an experiment, a data-quality flag, or a KB entry rather than prose that dies.
  Without this, the coach can be argued into agreement and change nothing, which is its own
  slow-burn disconnection: Mark learns that winning the argument does not matter. **Trigger:**
  a second instance of the coach conceding a real defect with no way to record it.
* **Response compression.** Noted by Batch 206 and still true: there is no `GZipMiddleware`
  anywhere in the API and nothing in `vercel.json`, so every response is served
  uncompressed. A cross-cutting win, unrelated to this wave. **Trigger:** its own batch.

## Post-roadmap — 2026-08-25 — Mark feedback 2026-08-21/24: the trend series reads a row that cannot hold the metric (Batches 225–227)

Craig relayed three points of Mark's feedback (2026-08-21 and 2026-08-24, sent before Batches
222–224 shipped but reconciled here against current `main` and against live production). One
is already fixed, one is a false statement the app is still making every time Mark opens
Trends, and one is a flag that has been firing six nights in seven without ever being wrong or
right.

**Every claim below was verified against production, not transcribed from the reports.** The
decisive evidence in each case is a number the app itself stored:

* **Mark's own diagnostic on the VO2 max gap was correct, and it points straight at the
  cause.** He wrote: "*oddly app always notes vo2 in post workout feedback so it is getting it
  but here it thinks it isn't.*" Both halves are true and they read different rows.
  `trends.py:756` collapses history with `index_morning_by_date`, while post-workout and chat
  call `resolve_effective_vo2max` (`body_metrics.py:69`), which scans a lookback window for the
  most recent non-null value regardless of phase. **VO2 max is a post-activity measurement:
  Garmin recomputes it after the ride, so it can never be on a wake row.** Production, by month
  and phase — July: 13 settled rows carry a reading, **0 of 30 morning rows do**; August: 13
  settled, **0 of 26 morning**. March–May survive only because no morning row exists yet to
  mask them, which is exactly why the report's spring numbers are right and its summer numbers
  are empty.
* **The failure is not a gap, it is an inversion.** True monthly means, settled-preferred:
  53.77 → 53.15 → 52.53 → 53.59 → **55.04 → 55.25**. Mark's VO2 max has risen roughly 2.7
  points off the May trough to the highest values in the window. The stored `seasonal_trend`
  packet behind his screenshot (generated 2026-08-21 09:37 UTC) holds `sampleCount: 0` and
  `currentMean: null` for both summer months and `status: insufficient_history` on the
  year-on-year row, so the model reported its packet faithfully. **The app told him a genuine
  training gain was a decline, and then told him twice that no conclusion could be drawn.**
  This is the false-fact failure Batch 214 named as worse than a missing one, and it is the
  third consumer-side casualty of the Batch 205 row split after Body Battery charge (216) and
  drain (224).
* **Point 2 is already fixed and was fixed after he wrote.** His 08-21 screenshot shows both
  Body Battery rows blank; PR #256 (`711055a`, Batch 224) landed 08-24. Verified live: the
  08-24 packet holds `currentValue: null` for both, and the **08-25 packet holds charge 65
  against median 68, delta −3**, with the basis line naming it as the overnight figure. Drain
  is still blank *by design* and now carries a visible `unavailableReason`. **His second
  sentence is the part that survives** — "*Does include figures in comments but no reference to
  vs median or trends*" — and it is measurably true: today's packet carries
  `yesterdayLoad.wholeDayCost.bodyBatteryDrained: 66` with `coverage: complete`, and his drain
  baseline is median 67, IQR 57.5–75. The figure and the baseline that explains it sit in the
  same packet, one join apart, and nothing joins them.
* **The REM flag has no personal reference because none exists to have.** REM is absent from
  `BASELINE_SPECS` (`sleep_history.py:84`) and from the trends `METRICS` registry
  (`trends.py:117`); the morning packet carries last night's `remSleepMin` and no history at
  all. The coach is asked to comment on a number it has no distribution for, against an age
  band of 15–23% (`age_norms.py:160`) — and across **428 nights Mark's median REM is 10.0%**
  (IQR 7.1–12.9), with **85% of nights below the band floor.** So "low REM" fires roughly six
  nights in seven and carries no information either way. Last night is the cost of that stated
  plainly: 74 min was far above his own p75, an unusually strong night, and the app had no way
  to tell him.

**Sequencing: 225 → 226 → 227.** 225 is the only one making Mark a false statement today. 226
is the smallest. 227 is the one he has now raised repeatedly and the only one that changes what
the coach can say about a metric it has been nagging him about for months.

### Recorded, not scheduled

* **A second profile can silently ingest the first user's Garmin data.** On 2026-08-24 19:22 a
  `Craig` profile (role `player`) appeared in production. Craig confirmed he did not create it;
  it was hard-deleted on 2026-08-25 (146 rows across 11 tables; backup at
  `~/craig-profile-backup-2026-08-25.json`). It had two activated devices, an imported
  58-workout plan, and generated its own morning brief — a second paid Claude call per day.
  **Nothing in the codebase can create a profile except `seeds.py`, which hardcodes
  `MARK_DISPLAY_NAME`;** `activate.py` raises "Active profile not found" rather than creating
  one, and no router or script constructs `Profile(...)`. So the row can only have come from
  direct SQL. The durable finding is the one underneath it: its `garmin_user_profile_pk` was
  `null`, yet it ingested Mark's Garmin history byte-for-byte, because sync keys off the single
  global tokenstore rather than the profile's own Garmin identity. **Trigger:** any real second
  user, which the product brief explicitly allows ("1–2 private users") — or a second profile
  reappearing without explanation, which would mean the creation path is not what this note
  says it is.

## Post-roadmap — 2026-08-26 — Found during Batch 227 closeout: the baselines nothing refreshes (Batch 228)

Batch 227 needed a `metric_baselines` backfill to land its new REM row, and running it surfaced
a defect larger than the missing row: **nothing in the system ever refreshes a personal
baseline.** `MetricBaselineBackfillService.rebuild` is called from exactly one place —
`src/metric_baselines_backfill.py`, a manual admin runner — and from nothing in the scheduler.

**Measured, not assumed.** `metric_baselines.created_at` records every refresh that has ever
happened, because each one only occurred when a human ran the script to add a *new* metric:
**2026-06-24** (seven rows), **2026-07-05** (`readiness_score`), **2026-08-20**
(`body_battery_drain`), **2026-08-26** (`rem_sleep_pct`, the Batch 227 closeout). The gaps are
11 days, **46 days**, and 6 days. Every refresh Mark has ever received was a side effect of
someone building something else. `job_runs` holds 14 distinct job names and not one of them
touches baselines.

**The cost is not hypothetical, and it lands on a safety gate.** The 2026-08-26 refresh moved
his readiness median **59.0 → 61.0**, and `effective_readiness_floor` is
`max(personal_center, 60)` (`personal_baselines.py:33`). So the floor moved **60.0 → 61.0**:
the stale baseline had been holding the soft-sleep Green gate **one point more permissive than
his own history warranted**. DECISIONS #249 exists to stop a *sinking* median making that rule
more permissive; nobody considered the inverse, where a *rising* median simply never arrives.
Three of nine medians moved on five days of new data (`age_adjusted_sleep_score` 79.5 → 81.0,
`readiness_score` 59.0 → 61.0, `sleep_score` 75.0 → 76.0), so the drift rate is real rather
than a rounding artefact.

**And the column that should have exposed this is lying.** `UpdatedAtMixin`
(`models/base.py`) declares `updated_at` with `server_default=func.now()` and **no
`onupdate`**, and no migration adds a trigger — so it never advances on UPDATE. Today's
backfill rewrote all nine rows (their `window_end_date` now reads 2026-08-25) while
`body_battery_drain.updated_at` still reads 2026-08-20 and seven others still read 2026-06-24.
The mixin is used by **17** model classes, so `updated_at` is a duplicate of `created_at` across
most of the schema. That is why the rot was invisible: the one field anyone would query to find
it cannot answer. **Corrected at `/batch-start`:** it is not even a faithful duplicate — on every
`metric_baselines` row `updated_at` is a few seconds *earlier* than `created_at`, because
`created_at` carries a Python-side `default=_utcnow` evaluated at flush while `updated_at` takes
Postgres `now()`, which is transaction-start time.

### Recorded, not scheduled (2026-08-26)

* **`updated_at` is a duplicate of `created_at` across most of the schema.** `UpdatedAtMixin`
  has no `onupdate` and no migration adds a trigger, so 17 model classes carry a column that
  looks like a freshness signal and is not one. Batch 228.2 only has to decide whether to
  *rely* on it; fixing it properly is a schema-wide change with a migration for existing rows
  and its own blast-radius audit — every query, packet field or admin view that reads
  `updated_at` is currently reading a creation date. **Trigger:** the first time anything needs
  to know when a row actually changed. Batch 228 was that moment for `metric_baselines`, and it
  answered the narrow question by *not* relying on the column: `unincorporated_nights` keys on
  `window_end_date`, which is already correct and honest. The schema-wide fix is still open.
* **~~The age-norm stage bands may be anchored to the wrong denominator.~~ Answered
  2026-08-26 — became Batch 229 below.** Mark's screenshots settled the denominator half
  (Garmin's displayed Duration excludes awake) but measurement then showed a naive swap makes
  three of four stage flags *worse*, so the band question stays closed and only the labelling
  half moves. See the section dated 2026-08-26 (Batch 229).

## Post-roadmap — 2026-08-26 — Mark's answer: the percentage he cannot reproduce (Batch 229)

Mark answered the sleep-stage question the same morning, with two screenshots of Garmin's own
Sleep screen for the night of **2026-08-26**. They settle the question, expose a gap in Batch
227's own delivery, and — usefully — kill the change that looked obvious.

**What the screenshots show.** Score 79 "Fair", Duration **7h 33m**, and the stage rows
Deep **1h 49m** (Excellent), Light **4h 56m** (Fair), REM **48m** (**Poor**), Awake/Restlessness
**35m** with 47 restless moments (Fair). Garmin's own headline for the night: *"Not enough REM —
you slept long enough, but your amount of REM sleep was low."*

**Garmin does not display stage percentages on that screen at all** — only durations and quality
words — so the question as asked could not be answered literally. The durations answer it anyway:
`1h49m + 4h56m + 48m = 7h33m`, **exactly** the displayed Duration, while adding the 35m awake
gives 8h08m and matches nothing. **Garmin's own total excludes awake — it is total sleep time.**
The stored `sleep` row matches the screenshots byte-for-byte on every field (score, qualifier,
all four stages, restless count, stress), so there is no data-quality gap to chase.

**The consequence is live and quotable.** The v37 brief generated for that night reads:

> **REM** came in at 48 minutes, which is **9.8% of sleep** — just below your own median of 9.9%
> … Garmin flags it against a younger-adult target (21–31%), but within the 50–59 age band the
> healthy range is 15–23%, so it sits below that too — though 21 of your last 28 nights have done
> the same. The chronic pattern is the real story here, not this individual night.

Batch 227's design works: his own distribution leads, the band is context, and the read does not
normalise the pattern away. But **"9.8% of sleep" is a figure Mark cannot reproduce from the
screen he reads.** 48m against his displayed 7h33m divides to **10.6%**; the app's 9.8% divides
by measured sleep, which includes 35 minutes of being awake — so the word "sleep" is doing work
the denominator does not support.

**This is a gap in Batch 227's own delivery, not a new discovery.** 227.3 required "pick one
definition, **state it where the number is surfaced**, and make both call sites use it." The
first and third shipped. The second did not: `REM_PCT_BASIS` exists in `age_norms.py` but is
referenced only by a code comment and two test assertions and **reaches no packet**, so the model
was handed a bare percentage and supplied its own noun. Worse, the test that was supposed to
cover this — `test_the_denominator_is_named_where_the_number_is_surfaced` — asserts only the
constant's own wording, so its name claims more than it checks.

**And the obvious fix is wrong.** Swapping the denominator to Garmin's Duration was measured
across 429 nights before being proposed, and it degrades three of the four stage flags, because
the Batch 61 bands and the measured-sleep denominator are a matched pair:

| stage | median (measured) | median (TST) | in-band now | in-band after | verdicts flipped |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Deep  | 15.92% | 17.26% | 211/429 | 214/429 | 51 (11.9%) |
| Light | 65.71% | 71.05% | **130/429** | **65/429** | 77 (17.9%) |
| REM   |  9.98% | 10.86% |  62/429 |  74/429 | 14 (3.3%) |
| Awake |  6.52% |  6.97% | 380/429 | 361/429 | 19 (4.4%) |

Light's in-band nights **halve**. On Mark's 2026-08-26 night specifically, Light flips from
"healthy for your age" (60.66%) to above the range (65.34%) on the denominator change alone.
Batch 227 was right to leave Batch 61 alone, and this row does the same.

| Batch | Tier | Status | Phases | Goal | Acceptance criteria |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~Batch 229 — A percentage says which total it is a percentage of~~ **(folded into Batch 230 at `/batch-start`, 2026-08-27 — do not build separately; 229.6's audit widened 230.3)** | 🟢 Mid | Folded into 230 | 229.1 **Finish the half of 227.3 that did not ship, and say that is what this is.** `REM_PCT_BASIS` reaches no packet; the model was given a bare number and wrote "9.8% of sleep" about a denominator containing 35 minutes of wakefulness. Rename or strengthen `test_the_denominator_is_named_where_the_number_is_surfaced`, which currently asserts only the constant's wording — the name overclaims and that is how the gap survived review.<br>229.2 **Re-confirm the two facts this rests on before building.** Garmin's displayed Duration excludes awake (Mark's 2026-08-26 screenshot: 1h49m + 4h56m + 48m = 7h33m exactly, and `duration_sec == deep+light+rem` on 245 of 428 stored nights, mean gap 11.5s). And the stored row still matches his screen field-for-field. If either has drifted, stop and re-measure — the whole row depends on the app and the watch describing the same night.<br>229.3 **Lead with the figure that needs no denominator.** 48 minutes reconciles with his screen exactly; the percentage never will while the totals differ. Make minutes the primary REM figure in the prose contract, with the percentage secondary **and carrying its basis** — Batch 217's convention, which already exists for exactly this ("a derived fact says how it was reached, in words Mark can read"). Emit `REM_PCT_BASIS` (or a shorter user-facing form of it) beside the number in the packet rather than leaving the noun to the model.<br>229.4 **Do not swap the denominator, and record why so it is not re-proposed.** The measured table above: Light's in-band nights halve from 130/429 to 65/429 and 77 verdicts flip; Deep flips 51. The Batch 61 bands were calibrated against measured sleep and are a matched pair with it. **This row changes labelling only.**<br>229.5 **State the condition under which the bands *could* move.** Only as a deliberate Batch 61 reopening that rebases the bands and the denominator **together**, with a new `DECISIONS.md` entry, and with awake handled separately — "awake as a percentage of total sleep time" is not a meaningful quantity, so a single global denominator cannot serve all four stages. Not this batch; written down so the next session inherits the reasoning rather than the conclusion.<br>229.6 **Consider whether the same gap exists elsewhere.** REM is the metric that got a basis constant and never surfaced it; check whether any other derived percentage or ratio in the morning packet reaches the prose without one, and fix or list them. Do not expand the batch to fix all of them — list what you find.<br>229.7 Tests: a packet case asserting the REM basis string is present beside the percentage; a prompt-contract case asserting minutes lead and the percentage carries its basis; a regression pinning the measured-sleep denominator (so 229.4 cannot be undone by accident); and a renamed/strengthened version of the 227 test that actually checks a packet rather than a constant. | Every REM figure Mark reads either matches his watch exactly or says plainly which total it is a share of, so he never has to work out why the app's percentage and his screen disagree. | The REM percentage never appears without its basis; minutes lead the read; the measured-sleep denominator is unchanged and pinned by a regression; and the 227 test no longer claims to check something it does not. **Priority note, honestly:** the number is not wrong, only unlabelled, and the prose already gives him the 48 minutes that do reconcile — so this is a credibility fix on a surface he has twice proved he audits, not a correctness one. |

### Recorded, not scheduled (2026-08-26, second pass)

* **Garmin and the app agree that this night's REM was low, by different routes — and Mark has
  now seen both.** Garmin's own screen says "Not enough REM … Poor" against its younger-adult
  target; the app says 9.8%, a near-median night *for him*, still below the 50–59 band, with
  **21 of his last 28 nights** the same. Batch 227's framing holds up under exactly the case that
  would embarrass it — it names the chronic pattern as the real story rather than explaining it
  away — but the two systems will keep producing differently-worded verdicts on the same night,
  and that is worth watching rather than fixing. **Trigger:** Mark asking why the app and his
  watch disagree about a night, which is the same question that produced Batches 225 and 229.

## Post-roadmap — 2026-08-26/27 — Mark's 08-26 wave: the figures he cannot reconcile, and the lever the data did not choose (Batches 230–231)

Three reports in one day, plus two defects found while reconciling them. **Every claim below was
measured against production or driven through the real code path; none is transcribed from a
report.** Two of the five are regressions from batches that shipped the same morning, and one is
the app stating the opposite of its own stored analysis.

* **The drain row costs six wrapped lines to say nothing.** Mark's screenshot of "Last night's
  metrics" shows Body Battery drain rendering an em dash plus Batch 224's explanation — *"still a
  part-day value at the morning sync; compare it with your full-day baseline after the day
  closes"* — making it **the tallest row in the table with no value in it**. The comparison it
  tells him to wait for is already on the same page, in prose Batch 226 shipped that morning:
  *"Body Battery drained 80 points yesterday, against your personal median of 67."* His ask is to
  swap them. A sharper point neither 224 nor 226 made: the table is headed **"Last night's
  metrics"** and the row describes **today**.
* **The tolerance is invisible, and it is not a bug.** `MetricComparisonTable.baselineDiff`
  applies `tol = max(|centre| × 0.03, 0.5)` before deciding "in range". On 2026-08-26: HRV
  current **50**, printed range **45–49**, centre 47, tol **1.41**, so the real bound is 50.41 and
  the row reads "in range" — a subtraction Mark can do. Readiness 76 against 42.8–71 correctly
  reads "5 above", so the two rows appear to contradict each other. **Second artefact:** the
  magnitude is measured from the quartile edge, not the tolerance bound, so readiness jumps from
  "in range" straight to "1.8 above" and can never read "1 above".
* **Batch 227 over-steered, in two places.** The instruction was *read against his own baseline
  first, and do not imply the band is a target he is failing*. What shipped reads closer to *the
  band barely matters*. In the table REM renders **"✓ in range" with no age frame at all**, while
  every other age-normed row carries both (`AGE_TO_BASELINE_KEY` deliberately omits REM because
  its age row lives in `sleepRows`). In the regenerated v6 trends narrative the same over-steer
  appears independently as *"not a deficit"*, *"not a structural concern"*, *"No concern here."* —
  against a morning brief, the same day, correctly calling it a chronic pattern with **21 of his
  last 28 nights** below the band, and against Garmin independently reporting *"Not enough REM —
  Poor."* The trends narrative also cites the wrong anchor: *"Against Mark's own baseline (median
  12.55% in March…)"* is simply the highest month in the window; his baseline is median **9.91**,
  IQR 7.50–13.12.
* **`REM_PCT_BASIS` reaches no packet, which is half of 227.3 unbuilt.** The constant exists and
  is referenced only by a code comment and two test assertions, so the model was handed
  `"rem_sleep_pct": 9.836065573770492` — raw float, no unit, no basis — and wrote **"9.8% of
  sleep"** about a total containing 35 minutes of wakefulness. Mark cannot reproduce that from his
  own screen (48m against a displayed 7h33m = **10.6%**). The test meant to cover it,
  `test_the_denominator_is_named_where_the_number_is_surfaced`, asserts only the constant's own
  wording — its name claims more than it checks.
* **The app told him the opposite of its own analysis.** Mark reported that the two REM questions
  are useless because he never trains late and always dims the lights, so neither can ever produce
  a not-applied night. He is right, and the reason is worse than he knew. The 2026-08-27
  `driver_correlation` analysis ranks his REM drivers: `bedroom_peak_fan_speed` **+0.345** (n=10),
  `sleep_stress_avg` −0.260 (n=121), `bedroom_warning_minutes` **−0.239** (n=64, *60+ min above
  19.5°C → −4.6 min REM*), `bedroom_critical_minutes` **−0.235** (n=64, *60+ min above 20°C →
  −11.1 min REM*), then bedroom min/mean/max temperature. **Six of the top eight REM drivers are
  bedroom thermal.** `prev_day_training_load` does not appear in REM's top eight at all; against
  sleep score it ranks 11th at **−0.046**. Yet the morning brief stated *"training load is the
  strongest measured lever, which the two active REM interventions this week are designed to
  address"*, and issued `late_training_guard` (whose `driver_affinity` is
  `{prev_day_training_load, resting_heart_rate_bpm}`) and `evening_light_down` (which has no
  affinity at all and therefore came from the blind weekly rotation). **The discovery Mark is
  asking for already exists, already targets REM, and already points at his bedroom.**


### Corrections made at `/batch-start 230` (2026-08-27), before any code

Every claim in the Batch 230 row was re-checked against current code and live production.
Five were wrong or incomplete; two of them change what gets built.

* **230.2's premise is false — it is a wiring fix, not a confirmation.** The row said
  "confirm `personalBaselines` reaches the trends packet". It does not: `trends.py:916`
  restricts `baseline_band_packet` to
  `keys={"readiness_score", "hrv_7_day_avg_ms", "resting_heart_rate_bpm"}`, so
  `rem_sleep_pct` is **absent from the trends packet entirely** while
  `TREND_SYSTEM_PROMPT` instructs the model to "interpret … REM against
  personalBaselines". Proof by contrast, in the v6 narrative itself: it quoted the
  readiness (mean 56.9 / median 61), RHR (median 44, IQR 43–45) and HRV
  (median 47, IQR 45–49, n=76) baselines **correctly**, and invented
  "median 12.55% in March" only for REM — the one metric it was told to use and not
  given. The anchor was not a tone failure; the model had nothing else to cite.
* **The REM baseline row was written by Batch 227's closeout backfill, not Batch 228's.**
  `metric_baselines.created_at` for `rem_sleep_pct` is 2026-08-26 06:13:57, the manual
  admin run. Batch 228's scheduled job fired for the first time at 2026-08-27 01:30 UTC
  and only *updated* it. (Values unchanged and re-confirmed: n=84, median 9.908,
  IQR 7.504–13.118, window 2026-06-04..2026-08-26.)
* **The unlabelled-denominator gap in 230.3 is not REM-only — all four stage
  percentages have it** (this is 229.6, folded in, and it changes the fix). The
  2026-08-27 brief printed Deep **17.3%**, Light **63.8%**, Awake **8.1%** and REM
  **10.9%** on four adjacent lines of one table. Every one is a share of *measured*
  sleep — 469 min including 38 min awake — and none says so; against Garmin's displayed
  431-min Duration none of the four reconciles. Naming the denominator on REM alone
  would leave three siblings with the identical defect beside it, so the basis is
  stated once for the stage group.
* **230.1's over-steer has since become a factual error, not a tone problem.** The
  2026-08-27 brief (v37, generated 07:43) wrote: *"The Garmin flag (it targets a
  younger-adult band of 15–23%) doesn't reflect your personal norm; Garmin's reference
  is calibrated against younger athletes."* **15–23% is the app's own Ohayon 50–59
  band.** Garmin's young-adult target is **21–31%**, and both sit in the same packet
  (`bandLow`/`bandHigh` against `garminTargetLow`/`garminTargetHigh`). The read
  mislabelled the app's own age band as Garmin's *in order to dismiss it*. The prompt
  rule therefore has to forbid the misattribution, not merely the conclusion.
* **229.2 re-measured, and 230.4 stands.** Across **430** stored nights (not 428),
  `duration_sec == deep+light+rem` exactly on **247**, is **never** equal to the
  awake-inclusive sum (0/430), mean gap 11.5 s. Median REM is **9.98%** of measured
  sleep against **10.87%** of Garmin's Duration. Mark's 2026-08-26 row still matches his
  screenshot field-for-field (79 FAIR, 27180 s, deep 6540 / light 17760 / REM 2880 /
  awake 2100, 47 restless), so 2880/27180 = **10.6%** on his screen against the app's
  2880/29280 = **9.84%**. The denominator is not swapped.

**Answered for free, and it belongs to Batch 231 — recorded, not built here.** 231.1 asked
whether the false *"training load is the strongest measured lever"* came from the model or
the wiring. **Neither branch it offered: the packet asserts the falsehood itself.**
`chronic_patterns._driver_for_flag:1348` hardcodes `prev_day_training_load` as the
preferred driver for `rem_sleep_pct` regardless of measured strength, and the emitted
`chronicSuggestions.items[0].summary` reads *"REM has repeatedly missed its age norm;
training load is the strongest measured lever to check first"* **while carrying
`coefficient: -0.0464, sampleCount: 115` in the same object**. The model invented nothing
and is not the defect. Two further facts the row does not have: it **recurred on
2026-08-27**, so it is not a one-off; and it reaches Mark through `ChronicSuggestionsCard`
on Home as well as the prose, so it is a rendered surface, not only a narrated one.

**Decisions taken at `/batch-start` (Craig's call), recorded so the reasoning outlives the
session:**

* **230.5 — the drain row is repointed at yesterday's closed day.** It renders yesterday's
  finished drain against the same baseline with a basis line naming the date, reusing the
  figure `_yesterday_load_packet` has already computed so the table and the prose cannot
  quote different numbers for one day. Batch 224's part-day withhold stays reachable for
  any surface that still shows today.
* **230.6 — the near-miss gets a name; the printed range stays the quartile range.**
  Strictly inside reads "in range"; inside the tolerance but outside the printed range
  reads a distinct near-miss. Magnitude keeps measuring from the printed quartile, so
  "5 above" remains 76−71 and Mark can still do the subtraction. Rejected: widening the
  printed range (it stops being his quartile range) and measuring from the tolerance
  bound (it trades a visible contradiction for an invisible one).
* **230.1 — the age frame goes on the REM row itself, on every surface.** Backend-derived
  from the same computed sleep row, so `MetricComparisonTable` and `SleepStageAgeTable`
  cannot drift. The mild repetition on `/sleep`, where both tables render, is the accepted
  trade: the row's job — stopping the personal frame from reading as the whole story —
  exists on every surface, and Batch 227's choice to omit it entirely is what produced
  this defect on two of the three.

| Batch 232 — A generation that runs for minutes cannot hold the door shut behind it | 🔴 High | Planned | 232.1 **Start from the lock, because it is the mechanism that turned one slow call into a morning with no brief.** `generation_requests.claim_generation_request:216` takes `pg_advisory_xact_lock` and holds it for the whole transaction — which includes the Anthropic call. On 2026-08-30 that call measured **75.1s**, and **139s** on regeneration. Of the seven failed attempts between 08:59 and 09:23, five died on `httpx.ReadTimeout` and **two died on `asyncpg.QueryCanceledError: canceling statement due to statement timeout` inside `claim_generation_request` itself** — they were not generating, they were *queued on the lock* and Postgres killed the wait at `statement_timeout`, measured at **2min** on this project. So a queued attempt does not wait, and does not get a useful error: it is cancelled, classified `other`, and shown to Mark as a generic retryable failure. **Do not fix this by lengthening `statement_timeout`** — that makes every queued attempt wait longer for the same answer. The lock exists to stop two workers generating one artifact; it does not need to be held while the *paid call* runs, only around the claim and the write. Establish at build time whether the claim can commit `RUNNING` and release the lock before generation, with the existing lease (not the lock) covering the worker-died case — that is what the lease is for, and `claim_generation_request`'s own comment at the `STATUS_RUNNING` branch already describes exactly that shape.<br>232.2 **The fix shipped on 2026-08-30 widened this window, deliberately, and the neighbours were not adjusted.** `fix/anthropic-read-timeout` raised the Anthropic read budget from a flat 60s to a per-phase 300s, because the brief genuinely needs minutes and every attempt that morning was throwing away a response Anthropic had already generated and billed. That was the right call and should not be reverted. But 300s is now **longer than the 120s `statement_timeout`** and **longer than `LEASE_DURATION = 3min` (180s)**, so two invariants that used to hold by accident no longer hold: previously the lock could be held at most ~60s and a queued attempt would usually acquire it before being cancelled; now a queued attempt is *guaranteed* to be cancelled at 120s while the holder generates. Reconcile the three numbers explicitly and write down the ordering they must keep, rather than leaving it to whoever next tunes one of them. **Note the 300s is env-tunable** (`ANTHROPIC_READ_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`), so the relationship must hold for the configured value, not the default.<br>232.3 **The pool is provisioned above the ceiling the pooler will grant.** `database.py:10` sets `pool_size=10, max_overflow=10`, so the app will open up to **20** connections; production runs against `aws-1-eu-north-1.pooler.supabase.com:5432` and Supavisor rejected the excess on 2026-08-30 with `(EMAXCONNSESSION) max clients reached in session mode - max clients are limited to pool_size: 15`. Observed **once** in the sampled log window, on a path other than the seven brief failures — so this is **observed, not proved to have cost Mark anything**, and it should be fixed on its own merits rather than credited with the outage. **Correct a stale comment while here:** `database.py:18` justifies `prepared_statement_cache_size=0` as "required for Supabase's transaction-mode pooler (port 6543)", but production is on **5432, session mode** — the comment describes a configuration this app does not use, and it is exactly the kind of pointer that sends the next reader the wrong way. Decide deliberately between sizing the pool under the session-mode ceiling and moving to 6543; the prerequisite for transaction mode (`prepared_statement_cache_size=0`) is *already* set, so that door is open, but session mode is what `pg_advisory_xact_lock` in 232.1 currently depends on — **a transaction-scoped advisory lock behaves differently under a transaction-mode pooler, so 232.1 and 232.3 must be decided together, not in either order alone.**<br>232.4 **Not a lock problem, but found in the same window and worth a decision: the daily-loop endpoint is slow.** Ten sampled `daily_loop served` lines on 2026-08-30 ranged **6.7s to 22.1s** total, with `envelope_ms` between 4.9s and 18.3s and `snapshot_ms` up to 10.1s. That is the request Mark's phone makes to open the app. No action is specified here — this phase is to **measure whether it is chronic or was contention from the retry storm**, and to record the answer, so a later batch is spending on evidence rather than on one bad morning.<br>232.5 **Recorded, not built — prompt caching cannot help a once-daily generation, and adding it would cost money.** Batch 231.7 flagged `cache_creation_input_tokens=0` / `cache_read_input_tokens=0` on the analytical paths, and 2026-08-30 reproduced it on the morning brief (`input_tokens=27830, output_tokens=3175`, both counters zero). **This is correct behaviour, not a defect.** The prompt cache TTL is 5 minutes by default and 1 hour at most; the morning brief runs once every 24 hours, so an entry written by one brief is always expired before the next could read it. Only `SYSTEM_PROMPT` is byte-stable anyway (18,044 chars, ~4.5k tokens = 16.2% of input) because `build_morning_user_prompt` serialises the whole packet as one `json.dumps(..., sort_keys=True)` blob with the `stamp_generation_identity` keys sorted in among it, leaving no stable prefix in the user turn. Adding `cache_control` to the brief would pay the ~1.25x write premium — **~$6.17/yr — to produce zero reads.** `brief_chat.py:552` already caches, correctly, on the one path where several calls share a prefix inside the TTL. **Trigger for revisiting:** a path that generates repeatedly within an hour off one prefix; not a zero counter on a daily job. For scale, the brief costs ~$0.131 per run (~$48/yr) and the input side is dominated by packet size, not by cache misses — **if brief cost is the concern, the lever is what goes in the packet, not caching.**<br>232.6 Tests: a case asserting a queued generation for the same scope does not sit inside `pg_advisory_xact_lock` while a paid call runs; a case pinning the ordering between the Anthropic read budget, `statement_timeout` and `LEASE_DURATION` so tuning one cannot silently invert it; a case asserting `pool_size + max_overflow` stays within the configured pooler ceiling; and the existing generation-request serialization guarantees (one artifact per scope, no double generation, lease recovers a died worker) still hold byte-for-byte. | One slow generation costs one slow generation — it does not cancel the attempts queued behind it, and the timeout, statement and lease budgets are ordered on purpose rather than by accident. | A queued attempt for the same artifact scope is never killed by `statement_timeout` while another generates; the Anthropic read budget, `statement_timeout` and `LEASE_DURATION` have a written, tested ordering that holds for the configured timeout and not just its default; `pool_size + max_overflow` cannot exceed the pooler ceiling, and the pooler-mode comment matches the port production actually uses; the advisory-lock and pooler-mode decisions are taken together; generation is still serialized per artifact scope with no double generation; and prompt caching is **not** added to the once-daily paths. |
