Confirm rather than assume: the count is what the guard reads, so a wrong assumption here is caught by the guard, not by Mark.<br>233.5 **Production runs on the code default, not on an env var — the review doc's "the swap is one env var" is true only once that variable exists.** The Railway `api` service sets `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and **nothing else Anthropic-related** — no `ANTHROPIC_MODEL`, no `ANTHROPIC_MAX_TOKENS` — so production is running `config.py`'s `claude-sonnet-4-6` at `4096`. Decide which lever ships: adding the Railway variable is revertible without a code deploy, changing the default is auditable in `git log`. **Correct `.env.example:87-88` either way** — it documents `ANTHROPIC_MAX_TOKENS=1600`, a value production does not use and which under Sonnet 5 would truncate the brief on its first run, so a fresh environment built from that file would fail immediately and for a reason the file itself caused.<br>233.6 **Re-derive the read timeout from the new ceiling, and do not tune it from two batches at once.** `config.py:56` sets `anthropic_read_timeout_seconds = 300.0`, and its own comment (`config.py:51-56`) derives that number from `anthropic_max_tokens` at ~15 tok/s. Raising `max_tokens` for thinking invalidates the derivation, and thinking adds generation time on top of it. **This collides with Batch 232.2 by construction**: 232 is reconciling the ordering between the Anthropic read budget, the 120s `statement_timeout` and the 180s `LEASE_DURATION`, and this batch changes the input that budget is derived from. Sequence behind 232, or state the ordering both must satisfy and pin it in the same test 232.6 adds — do not let two batches move one number independently.<br>233.7 **Confirm pricing live at build time; two figures in this repo already disagree.** `DECISIONS.md:947` (a live check made at `/batch-start 231` on 2026-08-27) records Sonnet 5 at **$2/$10 as the standard price** with the 2026-09-01 rise to $3/$15 cancelled, while `docs/claude-api-review.md:192` still describes $2/$10 as introductory **through 2026-08-31**. One of them is wrong; reconcile against the live pricing page and correct it. This is exactly the dated-fact trap 231.7 flagged and then survived by hedging, so hedge again. Either way the net is close to neutral — roughly 33% cheaper per token against roughly 30% more tokens — and Batch 232.5 measured the brief at **~$0.131/run (~$48/yr)** on Sonnet 4.6, so this batch should not be justified on cost.<br>233.8 **Prompt re-tuning is the actual work, and no unit test can verify it.** Sonnet 5 interprets instructions more literally than 4.6, so holdover style, tone and scope directives land at face value. The morning `SYSTEM_PROMPT` is **18,044 chars (~4.5k tokens, measured in 232.5)**, largely instruction about *not* softening deterministic verdicts — literalism makes those stronger, which is the safe direction — but "be concise"-class lines should be re-baselined rather than inherited. Verify by regenerating a small number of **real** days on both models and diffing the prose, not by fixture. **Do not let this batch also change what goes into the packet**: a prose diff is only readable if exactly one variable moved.<br>233.9 Tests: a boundary case proving `thinking` and `output_config` reach the payload when passed and are **absent** when not, so a caller that passes neither produces a byte-identical request to today's; a case covering whichever decision 233.2 takes for the two hardcoded ceilings; a case pinning that `stop_reason: "max_tokens"` still raises rather than returning partial text (the failure mode this batch exists to avoid must stay loud); a case pinning the read-budget ordering from 233.6 against the configured value rather than its default; and the eight existing callers unchanged where they pass nothing new. | The app runs on Sonnet 5 with room for the thinking it now does by default — and the swap is gated on a measured ceiling rather than shipped as a one-line config change that fails on its first morning brief. | A real production morning brief completes on `claude-sonnet-5` with `stop_reason: "end_turn"` and measured `output_tokens` inside the configured ceiling with headroom — proved by a live generation, not a fixture; the `brief_chat` (1024) and `conversation_learning` (1800) ceilings are decided explicitly rather than left below the new floor; `thinking` and `output_config` are reachable from `generate_anthropic_text` with the no-argument path byte-identical for the eight existing callers; no `temperature`/`top_p`/`top_k`/`budget_tokens`/prefill enters the payload; `.env.example` documents the values production actually uses; the read-timeout derivation is restated against the new ceiling and does not contradict Batch 232's ordering; current pricing is confirmed live and whichever of the two repo figures is wrong is corrected; and the deterministic verdict, the Batch 141 failure card and the batch path's `MAX_INPUT_TOKENS` guard are all unaffected. **Depends on Batch 232** — it owns the timeout/lease/statement ordering this batch perturbs. |
| Batch 234 — The client hung up on an answer Anthropic had already billed | 🔴 High | Built — not deployed | 234.1 **Authored retrospectively on 2026-08-30, after the fix was written — and authoring it surfaced what the fix's own commit message got wrong: it is not deployed.** Production `/api/v1/health` serves `c4a1161`, which is `origin/main`'s tip and the Batch 231 closeout; `152c792` is contained by **no remote branch**. Railway deploys from `main` (Decision #39), so **the flat 60s timeout is still live in production** and the 2026-08-30 outage cause is unfixed. The commit's closing line — *"Today's brief has been regenerated against prod and is live"* — is true of the **artifact**, which was regenerated with `railway run` against local code, and false of the **fix**. Status is `Built — not deployed` rather than `Shipped` for exactly that reason: every other Shipped row in this ledger carries a squash SHA verified serving from `/api/v1/health`, and this one cannot until it is merged. **Until then the next morning brief is expected to fail the same way.**<br>234.2 **What happened.** Seven attempts between **08:59 and 09:23** on 2026-08-30 failed identically: `httpx.ReadTimeout` out of `generate_anthropic_text`, classified `other`, surfaced to Mark as a retryable failure card that then failed again on every retry. He had no brief that morning.<br>234.3 **The cause was the client — not Anthropic, and not the data.** `httpx.AsyncClient(timeout=60.0)` applied a flat 60s to *every* phase, so the read budget was really "60s for Claude to finish the whole answer". Measured against prod that day the call takes **75.1s** (27.7k in / 2.8k out at ~38 output tok/s) and **139s** on regeneration; the request succeeds with `end_turn` every time and the client hangs up before reading it — **Anthropic had already generated, and billed, work that was then discarded.** Nothing broke on 08-30: the packet simply grew past a ceiling that had been sitting under it all along. The user prompt is now ~63k chars, so it would have failed every morning after.<br>234.4 **The fix: per-phase budgets, not a bigger flat number.** `read` scales with generation length — **300s**, since `anthropic_max_tokens` at a slow ~15 tok/s is ~275s, and still half the Anthropic SDK's own 600s default — while `connect`/`write`/`pool` stay short (10s/30s/10s) so a genuinely unreachable API still fails fast instead of hanging for the full read budget. Env-tunable via `ANTHROPIC_READ_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` so a slow spell can be ridden out without a deploy. Covers **all eight** analysis callers of the shared boundary, not just the morning brief — the same ceiling sat under post-workout, chat and the reviews.<br>234.5 **Deliberately out of scope: the Batches control plane.** `anthropic_batch.py`'s 60s clients are untouched, because submit/poll/retrieve are control-plane calls rather than generation — a long read budget there would hide a stuck poll instead of accommodating a long answer.<br>234.6 **What it did not fix, which is why two batches descend from it.** The fix closed the cause and **widened a window it did not create**: 300s is now longer than the 120s `statement_timeout` and the 180s `LEASE_DURATION`, so **Batch 232** owns reconciling that ordering and the advisory lock held across the paid call. Separately the read budget is *derived from* `anthropic_max_tokens`, which **Batch 233** must raise to make room for Sonnet 5's adaptive thinking. Both successors move numbers this batch set, and neither should move them alone.<br>234.7 Tests shipped with the fix: 80 lines added to `test_anthropic_text.py` covering per-phase timeout construction and the `ANTHROPIC_READ_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` override (`test_anthropic_text.py:312` monkeypatches it to 450.0). **Recorded, not fixed here:** the review in `docs/claude-api-review.md` finds the boundary still has *no test that exercises an actual timeout*, and still does not catch `httpx.TimeoutException` — so a `ReadTimeout` escapes as a generic exception rather than an `AnthropicApiError` with `reason="timeout"`, which is why all seven attempts were classified `other`. The classification defect that made the outage illegible is still open. | Mark's morning brief stops failing on a client-side ceiling that was smaller than the answer it asked for — and the ledger stops having a production incident fix with no row. | Retrospective row; the work is done and committed as `152c792`. **Delivered:** per-phase timeout budgets across all eight generation callers, an env-tunable read budget, 80 lines of new boundary tests, and the 2026-08-30 brief regenerated against prod and live. **Outstanding, and the reason this is not `Shipped`:** the fix is not in production — `152c792` is on no remote branch, prod serves `c4a1161`, and Railway deploys from `main`. This becomes Shipped when a merge SHA is verified serving from `/api/v1/health`; until then the 60s ceiling is live and the outage can recur on any morning. The timeout **classification** gap (234.7) is explicitly not closed by this batch. |

### Corrections made at `/batch-start 231` (2026-08-28), before any code

Seven of the row's claims were checked against current code and the stored production
packets. Four were wrong or incomplete, and three of those changed what got built.

* **The drivers offered to a REM flag were never REM's — this is the defect 231.1 was
  looking for, one layer below where it looked.** All three consumers read
  `drivers_report.outcomes.get(OUTCOME_SLEEP_SCORE)` (`daily_loop.py:1368` and `:1395`,
  `morning_analysis.py:599`, `sleep_projection_context.py:106`), so even with the
  `prev_day_training_load` hardcode removed the card would still have quoted the strongest
  driver of a **different outcome**. The `coefficient: -0.0464, sampleCount: 115` in the
  emitted summary is the *sleep score* figure and ranks **12th of 13, not 11th**; against
  REM's own ranking it is also 12th of 13 (−0.0783). It is close to the weakest measured
  thing in the whole set on either outcome.
* **231.2's answer: the REM ranking reaches no surface at all.** `insights.py` computes all
  four outcomes and caches them daily (47 `driver_correlation` rows), but the morning packet
  has **no driver key** — `chronicSuggestions.items[].driver` is the only driver that reaches
  Mark anywhere. No web page calls `/api/v1/insights/*`, and `driver_sentence` returns `None`
  for every driver except the four bedroom keys, so REM's #2 driver (`sleep_stress_avg`,
  n=121) has no readable sentence either. The routing stop is one line, not a pipeline.
* **Three drivers cannot be levers, and one was measured *after* the outcome it explained.**
  `bedroom_*` keys are aligned to the night before the wake date and `prev_day_training_load`
  to the evening before, but `sleep_stress_avg` comes off the same night's sleep row,
  `resting_heart_rate_bpm` off the wake-morning row, and `daytime_stress_avg` read the
  settled aggregate of the **whole day after the night**. Since `sleep_stress_avg` is REM's
  second-strongest driver at **−0.2597 on n=121**, "just pick the strongest" would have
  swapped one false lever claim for another. The stress driver is renamed
  `prev_day_stress_avg` and re-aligned to day−1; the other two are classified as concurrent
  and can never be named as levers.
* **It fired four mornings, not once, and it is the only suggestion he gets.** 2026-08-25 to
  **2026-08-28 inclusive** — including today, under the v39 prompt that shipped hours
  earlier, at −0.0856. REM is the sole `chronicSuggestions` item on every one of those days,
  rendered on Home (`SleepSnapshotBody` → `DashboardPage.tsx:639`) and on `/sleep`
  (`SleepPage.tsx:190`) as well as narrated.
* **231.3 is partly stale: the thermal drivers already had a matching lever.**
  `room_cool_late_cycles` carried `bedroom_warning_minutes`, `bedroom_critical_minutes`,
  `bedroom_fan_ran_minutes`, `bedroom_peak_fan_speed` and `overnight_low_c` before this
  batch — it was simply never reached, because `_driver_for_flag` returned the load driver
  first. The real gaps were `bedroom_mean/min/max_temp_c` (no lever at all) and
  `resting_heart_rate_bpm` mapping to `late_training_guard`, which is the same category
  error the row names for load. A test now asserts the classification partitions
  `DRIVER_KEYS`, so a new driver cannot be added without deciding whether it is a lever.
* **231.4's "nothing records his standing habits" is wrong, and the truth is worse.** Batch
  151 built `learned_context` — a versioned KB section, packet-wired, guard-railed. In
  production it holds `{"items": []}`, `conversation_learning_proposals` has **zero rows**,
  and no scheduler job runs the extraction (15 job names in `coach.job_runs`, none for
  learning), so nothing has ever proposed anything. Separately, what Mark has actually stated
  repeatedly in `brief_messages` is **alcohol** — *"I rarely drink"* (08-08), *"only … very
  occasional social events probably six times a year"* (08-20), plus 08-07 and 08-09 — not
  light-dimming; the nearest light statement is *"I usually read for around 15 mins"*
  (08-20). **Craig's call at `/batch-start`:** a habit that suppresses an issued lever is a
  coaching decision, so it gets its own deterministic `standing_habits` section rather than
  gating advice on model-extracted text, which Decision #228 explicitly forbids.
* **231.7 — prompt caching is not broken, it was never switched on, and the pricing fact the
  row flagged has inverted.** `cache_control` is set in exactly one file,
  `brief_chat.py:552`; `anthropic_text.py` supports a system-block breakpoint and reports the
  counters but no analytical path passes one, which is why all four regenerations on 08-28
  logged zeros. The row was right to say "confirm current pricing at build time": the live
  pricing page now states that Sonnet 5's **$2/$10 is the standard price and the increase to
  $3/$15 on 2026-09-01 will not occur**, so the deadline the row hedged against is gone.

**Decisions taken at `/batch-start` (Craig's call), recorded so the reasoning outlives the
session:**

* **231.4 — a habit lives in a new deterministic `standing_habits` KB section**, seeded
  empty, human-written, read by the lever issuer. `learned_context` stays what Batch 151
  made it: quoted data the model may read and nothing may act on. Rejected: gating advice on
  `learned_context` (needs a decision overriding #228) and inferring compliance from check-in
  responses (needs him to answer the unanswerable question three times first).
* **231.6 and 231.7 are recorded, not built.** The batch ships 231.1–231.5 and 231.8. The
  model-nominates/Python-tests rule is written into `DECISIONS.md` without building a
  nomination pipeline, and 231.7's measurements and pricing are recorded with a proposed
  per-job map but no code change — the defect fix is large enough on its own, and a
  model-config change touching all eight generation call sites deserves its own gate.

### Recorded, not scheduled (2026-08-27)

* **The morning brief and the trends narrative can contradict each other, and nothing stops
  them.** 230.7 adds a contract test for REM specifically, but the underlying gap is general:
  two prompts read overlapping packets and produce independent prose, with no shared statement
  of what a metric currently means. REM is simply where it became visible, because Batch 227
  changed the wording on one surface and not the other. **Trigger:** a second metric described
  differently on two surfaces on the same day — at which point the fix is a shared derived
  statement rather than another per-metric test.
